Charged scalar fields on Reissner–Nordström spacetimes I: integrated energy estimates
Dejan Gajic ∗ 1

Abstract
. This is the first part of a series of papers deriving the precise, late-time behaviour and (in)stability properties of charged scalar fields on near-extremal Reissner–Nordström spacetimes via energy estimates. In this paper, we establish global, weighted integrated energy decay and energy boundedness estimates for solutions to the charged scalar field equation on (near-)extremal Reissner–Nordström(–de Sitter) spacetimes. These estimates extend to Reissner–Nordström spacetimes away from extremality under the assumption of mode stability on the real axis. Together with the companion paper [ Gaj26 ] , this paper forms the first global quantitative analysis of the charged scalar field equation on asymptotically flat black hole spacetimes, without a smallness assumption on the scalar field charge. Due to a coupling of the degeneration of the red-shift effect with the presence of superradiance at the linearized level, charged scalar fields on Reissner–Nordström spacetimes also probe some of the main difficulties encountered when studying the (neutral) wave equation on extremal Kerr spacetimes.



1.
Introduction

Integrated energy estimates are a core ingredient for deriving global decay results for nonlinear, geometric wave equations. Schematically, they take the following form:

\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}w_{1}|\partial\phi|^{2}+w_{2}|\phi|^{2}\,d\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}}d\tau\leq\int_{\Sigma_{0}}\tilde{w}_{1}|\partial\phi|^{2}+\tilde{w}_{2}|\phi|^{2}\,d\mu_{\Sigma_{0}}+\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}(\hat{w}_{1}|\partial\phi|+\hat{w}_{2}|\phi|)\cdot|\mathcal{L}\phi|\,\,d\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}}d\tau,

(1.1)

with
\mathcal{L}
a linearized wave operator,
\{\Sigma_{\tau}\}_{\tau\in[0,\infty)}
a foliation by spacelike hypersurfaces,
w_{i},\tilde{w}_{i},\hat{w}_{i}:\Sigma_{\tau}\to\mathbb{R}
weight functions with possible degeneracies.

Estimates of the above type with
\mathcal{L}=\square
, the standard wave operator, originate in the work of Morawetz
[
Mor68
]
, where they were used to study nonlinear wave equations on the Minkowski spacetime.

In the context of asymptotically flat black hole spacetimes, the nature and validity of (
1.1
) is intimately related to the following obstructions to decay that each have an analogue in the dynamics of null geodesics:

(A)

degeneration of energy control near trapped null geodesics,

(B)

superradiance,

(C)

degeneration of red-shift near the event horizon,

(D)

degeneration of red-shift near null infinity.
1
1

1

This may be interpreted as the degeneration of the red-shift effect along the future cosmological horizon
\mathcal{C}^{+}
as the cosmological constant
\Lambda
goes to
0
, with future null infinity
\mathcal{I}^{+}
playing the role of
\mathcal{C}^{+}
in the limit. The existence of hierarchies of
r^{p}
-weighted energy estimates in the far-away region, see
[
DR10b
]
, may be thought of as a “degenerate red-shift effect”.

The above mechanisms all act as obstructions for proving decay, and they may be (strongly) coupled. In particular, if there exist trapped null geodesics that remain trapped under generic perturbations in phase space (stably trapped null geodesics), see
[
Mos16
,
Bur98
]
, or if there exists superradiance without an event horizon, see
[
Mos18
]
, then an estimate of the form (
1.1
) fails to hold.

Extremal black holes feature a coupling of (a subset) of (A)–(D), which is delicately balanced so that the validity of (
1.1
) cannot immediately be ruled out, in contrast with the examples in the above paragraph, but it is significantly harder to establish compared to the sub-extremal black hole setting, where only (A), (B) and (D) are present and they are moreover decoupled. This is closely related to the critical nature of extremal black holes as members of families of sub-extremal stationary black holes.

Not all extremal black holes feature a coupling between all the phenomena (A)–(D). For example, in the case of the wave equation on extremal Kerr black holes (A)–(C) are coupled, but (D) is decoupled.

In the case of the (neutral) wave equation on extremal Reissner–Nordström, (B) is not present and (A), (C) and (D) are all decoupled.

In the present paper, we will investigate a setting which features all the difficulties (A)--(D) and in which (B)--(D) are moreover coupled.
2
2

2

While trapping of null geodesics is present and more complicated than in the extremal Reissner–Nordström case, it can be decoupled from the remaining difficulties.

We consider the conformally covariant, inhomogeneous charged scalar field equation on Reissner–Nordström spacetime backgrounds:

\displaystyle(g^{-1}_{M,Q})^{\mu\nu}\>(^{A}D)_{\mu}\>(^{A}D)_{\nu}\phi=G_{A},

(1.2)

where
g_{M,Q}
is the Reissner–Nordström metric and
{}^{A}D=\nabla-i\mathfrak{q}A\otimes(\cdot)
is the electromagnetic gauge derivative, with
A
a 1-form and
dA=F_{Q}=-Q^{2}r^{-2}dt\wedge dr
the Reissner–Nordström Faraday tensor.

While our main interest are the
extremal
Reissner–Nordström (
|Q|=M
) and
near
-extremal Reissner–Nordström spacetimes (
0<1-\frac{|Q|}{M}\ll 1
), we also establish conditional results for the remaining members of the Reissner–Nordström family. Furthermore, nearby members of the sub-extremal Reissner–Nordström–
de Sitter
family also play a role in the analysis.

The present paper is the first part of a series of papers concerning charged scalar fields on Reissner–Nordström black holes. A precise late-time analysis of the decay behaviour of solutions to (
1.2
) is carried out in the companion paper
[
Gaj26
]
, using the results of the present paper as a starting point.

The main motivation for considering (
1.2
) is two-fold:

•

With the restriction of spherical symmetry, equation (
1.2
) is the linearization of the spherically symmetric Einstein–Maxwell–charged scalar field system of equations. A robust analysis of its linearization is the first step towards an analysis of 1) the nonlinear coupled Maxwell–charged scalar field equations, and then 2) the full Einstein–Maxwell–charged scalar field system. Already in spherical symmetry, we expect that the tools developed in the present paper and the companion paper
[
Gaj26
]
will play an important role when studying the Einstein–Maxwell–charged scalar field system, in particular near extremality.

•

The difficulties encountered in (
1.2
) resemble closely the difficulties encountered in the study of the wave equation on extremal Kerr. In future work, the methods introduced in the present paper will be used to sharpen the instability results in
[
Gaj23a
]
and upgrade them to a full understanding of the late-time asymptotic behaviour of fixed azimuthal modes. This leaves open an understanding of the infinite sum of azimuthal modes, which is also expected to make use of the ideas developed in the present paper.

In the present paper, we prove energy boundedness and (global) integrated energy estimates in the following form:

Theorem 1.1

(Energy boundedness and integrated energy estimates; informal version of Theorem
3.1
)
.

Let
\epsilon>0
. Assume that
0\leq 1-\frac{|Q|}{M}\ll 1
or
|\mathfrak{q}Q|\ll 1
. Write:

G_{A}=(g^{-1}_{M,Q})^{\mu\nu}\>(^{A}D_{\mu})\>(^{A}D_{\nu})(r^{-1}\psi).

Then there exists a uniform constant
C>0
such that for all
\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}
:

\sup_{\tau\in[\tau_{1},\tau_{2}]}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\mathcal{E}_{1-\epsilon}[\psi]\,r^{-2}d\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}}+\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\left[\upzeta(\rho_{+}+\kappa_{+})r^{-1}\mathcal{E}_{1-\epsilon}[\psi]+(1-\upzeta)r^{-2}|\psi|^{2}\right]\,r^{-2}d\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}}d\tau\\
\leq C\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{1}}}\mathcal{E}_{1+\epsilon}[\psi]\,r^{-2}d\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\\
+C\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\left[(\Omega^{-1}r)^{1+\epsilon}\rho_{+}^{1-2\epsilon}r^{-1+2\epsilon}|r^{2}G_{A}|^{2}+(1-\upzeta)|^{A}D_{\tau}G_{A}|^{2}\right]r^{-2}\,d\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\,d\tau,

(1.3)

where:

•

\Sigma_{\tau}
are horizon-intersecting, asymptotically hyperboloidal level sets of the time function
\tau
with induced volume for
d\mu_{\tau}
, equipped with coordinates
(r,\theta,\varphi)
, where
r
is the standard radius function,

•

\Omega^{2}=1-\frac{2M}{r}+\frac{Q^{2}}{r^{2}}
,

•

r_{+}
is the event horizon radius and
\rho_{+}(r)=r_{+}^{-1}-r^{-1}
,

•

\kappa_{+}
is the surface gravity of the event horizon,

•

\mathcal{E}_{p}[\psi]
are weighted energy densities, which can be uniformly estimated follows:

\mathcal{E}_{p}[\psi]\sim(\Omega^{-1}r)^{p}\Omega^{2}|^{A}D_{r}\psi|^{2}+r^{-2}(|^{A}D_{\tau}\psi|^{2}+|^{A}D_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\psi|^{2}+|\psi|^{2}),

•

\upzeta
is a cut-off function that is identically 1 outside of a small neighbourhood of
\{r=r_{\sharp}\}
, the photon sphere, and is identically zero inside an even smaller neighbourhood of
\{r=r_{\sharp}\}
.

The estimate holds also when
1-\frac{|Q|}{M}\gtrsim 1
and
|\mathfrak{q}Q|\gtrsim 1
, under the assumption of
mode stability on the real axis
.

Theorem
1.1
may be thought of as a
weak
energy decay statement, which forms a crucial ingredient in the proof of
strong
energy decay statements, precise late-time asymptotics and instabilities in the companion paper
[
Gaj26
]
; see
[
Gaj26
]
[Theorems 1.1 and 1.2]. A precise version of Theorem
1.1
can be found in the statement of Theorem
3.1
.

We now provide some remarks.

Remark 1.1

(Coupling between Morawetz and
(\Omega^{-1}r)^{p}
-weighted energy estimates)
.

The integrated estimate (
1.3
) should be interpreted as a combination of a local (in space) integrated energy estimate, or Morawetz estimate, with additional
(\Omega^{-1}r)^{p}
-weighted energy estimates near the horizons/null infinity, in the spirit of
[
DR10b
]
and
[
AAG20
]
.

Two related difficulties that are particular to the charged scalar field setting are: 1) the need for an energy flux
\mathcal{E}_{p}[\psi]
on the right-hand side with
p>0
(even in the small
|\mathfrak{q}Q|
setting), 2) for sufficiently large
|\mathfrak{q}Q|
, we can only establish
(\Omega^{-1}r)^{p}
-weighted energy estimates near the horizons that are uniform in
\kappa_{+}
and
\kappa_{c}
as
\kappa_{+}\downarrow 0
and
\kappa_{c}\downarrow 0
as part of a global analysis in frequency space. In this sense, the difficulties (C) and (D) introduced above become strongly coupled with (A) and (B) when
|\mathfrak{q}Q|
is sufficiently large.

Remark 1.2

(Loss of weights in energy boundedness)
.

Note the loss of an
\epsilon
-power in the
\mathcal{E}_{p}[\psi]
-integrals along
\Sigma_{\tau_{i}}
when comparing the two sides of (
1.3
). This loss can be removed by restricting to
|\mathfrak{q}Q|<\frac{1}{4}
; see the precise statement of Theorem
3.1
, using the fact that (C) and (D) decouple from (A) and (B) in that case. Alternatively, one can fix an arbitrarily
|\mathfrak{q}|
, but restrict to
\psi
supported on sufficiently large angular frequencies,
\psi_{\geq\ell}
, to remove the
\epsilon
-loss (and also increase the range of
|p|
).

Note also that an analogue of (
1.3
) can be derived without the above
\epsilon
-loss, using purely physical-space-based methods, but
including
spacetime integrals of zeroth order terms on the right-hand side (that are moreover supported away from the photon sphere); see Corollary
A.2
. This reflects the fact that the
\epsilon
-loss may be thought of as a
low-frequency
phenomenon.

Integrated energy estimates (modulo lower-order terms) obtained purely from an application of the divergence theorem with appropriate energy currents in physical space are relevant when extending the analysis from linear wave equations to quasilinear wave equations, and are therefore relevant for the Einstein equations. We refer the reader to the philosophy introduced in
[
DHRT22
,
DHRT24
]
for more details.

Remark 1.3

(Small
|\mathfrak{q}|
)
.

In the special case that
\mathfrak{q}=0
, (
1.3
) follows straightforwardly from by generalizing the methods valid in the
G_{A}=0
case (see for example
[
DR09
]
for the sub-extremal setting and
[
Are11
]
for the extremal setting). In the case
|\mathfrak{q}Q|\ll 1
we moreover prove an analogue of (
1.3
) without resorting to Fourier transforms in time; see Corollary
4.2
. This can be interpreted as the statement that the coupling between (B), (C) and (D) is weak for very small
|\mathfrak{q}|
.
The bulk of the analysis in the present paper is required to treat the case where
|\mathfrak{q}Q|
is
not
small!

Remark 1.4

(Mode stability)
.

Note that Theorem
1.1
only applies
unconditionally
to Reissner–Nordström solutions with
0\leq 1-\frac{|Q|}{M}\ll 1
(or alternatively, with
\frac{|Q|}{M}\leq 1
, but
|\mathfrak{q}Q|\ll 1
). Outside of this parameter range, the estimate becomes
conditional
on the validity of
mode stability on the real axis
(away from
\omega=0
). Qualitatively, this corresponds to the non-existence of purely oscillating, finite energy solutions, which remans an open problem on the full sub-extremal Reissner--Nordström family.
3
3

3

Note that, under the assumption of mode stability on the real axis, Theorem
1.1
implies mode stability in the upper-half complex plane, as it excludes the existence of exponentially growing modes.

Indeed, currently available methods in the literature for establishing mode stability
[
Whi89
,
SR15
,
TdC20
,
CT22
]
apply only in a restricted frequency range when
\frac{|Q|}{M}
is strictly bounded away from extremality, see Proposition
B.1
, but they fail to hold for all superradiant frequencies
4
4

4

For non-superradiant frequencies, mode stability follows straightforwardly; see for example Lemma
7.13
.
. Fortuitously, the methods of
[
TdC20
]
do remain applicable in the
extremal
Reissner–Nordström case, which, as we will show, implies also mode stability for sufficiently small
\kappa_{+}
(and also for Reissner–Nordström–de Sitter with sufficiently small
M^{2}\Lambda>0
).
5
5

5

Even if oscillating or exponentially growing modes are present, one could in principle apply the methods in
[
GW21
,
GW24a
]
to consider a finite-codimension subset of initial data that
excludes
these modes from being excited in the time evolution.

Let us also that in
[
BH21
]
, it was shown that there exist exponentially growing modes for the charged Klein–Gordon equation on Reissner–Nordström–de Sitter with
\frac{2}{3}\Lambda
replaced by
\mathfrak{m}^{2}(\Lambda)
, and
\mathfrak{m}^{2}(\Lambda)
sufficiently small depending on
\Lambda
(together with a corresponding largeness assumption on
|\mathfrak{q}Q|
).

Remark 1.5

(The role of
\Lambda
)
.

Solutions to the conformally covariant charged scalar field equation:

(g^{-1}_{M,Q,\Lambda})^{\mu\nu}\>(^{A}D)_{\mu}\>(^{A}D)_{\nu}\phi-\frac{2\Lambda}{3}\phi=G_{A},

with respect to Reissner–Nordström–de Sitter metrics
g_{M,Q,\Lambda}
with small
M^{2}\Lambda
play an important role for establishing (
1.3
) in the
\Lambda=0
case. More precisely, the positive surface gravity of the cosmological horizon
\kappa_{c}>0
(as well as
\kappa_{+}>0
) is used to derive future-integrability in time of solutions to (
1.2
) with an appropriately decaying
G_{A}
. Future-integrability is a property that is initially
assumed
to be able to carry out an analysis in Fourier space, but needs to be justified in order to complete the argument and conclude (
1.3
). This is first done via a continuity argument in the charge parameter
\mathfrak{q}
in the
\kappa_{+},\kappa_{c}>0
setting, making use of the red-shift effect. One may compare this with
[
DRSR16
]
where future-integrability is instead justified in the context of neutral scalar fields on sub-extremal Kerr via a continuity argument in the Kerr rotation parameter
a
.

After justifying the future-integrability assumption in the setting where
\kappa_{+}>0
and
\kappa_{c}>0
, we use the uniformity of the estimates in
\kappa_{+},\kappa_{c}
to be able to take the limit
\kappa_{+}\downarrow 0
and
\kappa_{c}\downarrow 0
and conclude (
1.3
). As part of this argument, we show that an analogue of Theorem
1.1
holds for
0<M^{2}\Lambda
suitably small (uniformly in
\Lambda
). The cosmological constant
\Lambda
therefore has a regularizing effect. It plays a similar regularizing role in the construction of quasinormal modes in
[
GW21
]
. See also the uniform-in-
\Lambda
analysis in
[
FST26
]
.

Remark 1.6

(Electromagnetic gauge)
.

Note that (
1.3
) is
gauge-invariant
, in the sense that it is independent of the particular choice of electromagnetic gauge
A
, provided we multiply
G_{A}
by an appropriate phase factor; see §
2.8
. It is convenient to consider the (conformally) irregular 1-form

A=\widetilde{A}=-\frac{Q}{r}dt,

in the Fourier analysis, while the (conformally) smooth 1-form

A=\widehat{A}=-\frac{Q}{r}d\tau.

is more convenient to establish local physical-space-based energy estimates.

1.1.
Previous results

In this section, we give an overview of related previous work concerning integrated energy estimates for the wave equation on black holes spacetimes. We provide further references pertaining to precise, late-time (pointwise) decay estimates in our companion paper
[
Gaj26
]
.

1.1.1.
Integrated energy estimates on sub-extremal black holes

In the context of Schwarzschild spacetimes, establishing
local
integrated energy estimates (in a spatially bounded region, supported away from the event horizon) requires an understanding of the obstruction of (unstable) trapped null geodesics at the photon sphere
\{r=3M\}
. Integrated local energy estimates for the wave equation on Schwarzschild were first obtained in
[
DR09
]
without a decomposition into time frequencies
\omega
, requiring merely a decomposition into angular frequencies
\ell
. See also the related earlier work
[
BS03
]
, as well as
[
MMTT10
]
for a refined understanding of the effects of trapped null geodesics. Note that the decomposition into angular frequencies in proofs of local integrated energy estimates was removed entirely in
[
HMV24
]
.

An important ingredient towards turning the
local
integrated energy estimates into
global
integrated energy estimates that extend to the horizon and future null infinity are
red-shift estimates
(near the horizon), introduced in
[
DR09
]
and
r^{p}
-weighted energy estimates
(in a far-away region), introduced in
[
DR10b
]
. As will be clarified below,
r^{p}
-weighted energy estimates may be viewed as a degenerate analogue of red-shift estimates.

In going from Schwarzschild to the more general Kerr setting, one faces several important additional difficulties. First of all, due to the presence of an ergoregion, where the Killing vector fields generating time translation symmetry
\partial_{t}
fail to be timelike, one cannot prove an energy boundedness statement independently from an integrated energy estimate, in contrast with the Schwarzschild setting. When decomposing into time frequencies
\omega
, this manifests itself as
superradiance

[
Zel71
,
Zel72
]
, see §
1.2.2
for a discussion of superradiance in the charged setting. In addition, the existence of trapped null geodesics arises creates an obstruction at a range of
r
-values when varying the time frequency, rather than a single
r
-value, as in Schwarzschild.

Integrated energy estimates in the full sub-extremal Kerr range were first obtained in
[
DRSR16
]
by carrying out a full decomposition into time frequencies and oblate spheroidal harmonic modes (appropriate generalizations of spherical harmonic modes) and treating different time- and angular-frequency regimes separately. An important step in the proof is a proof of
future-integrability
of solutions, which is necessary to be able to apply the estimates at the level of the Fourier transform in time. See also the estimates in
[
DHRT24
]
, which only rely on a decomposition into time frequencies and azimuthal modes, at the expense of introducing lower-order terms on the right-hand side. We also point the reader to the comprehensive analysis on integrated energy estimates in setting of the Teukolsky equation in
[
SRT20
,
SRT23
]
and earlier works in the very slowly rotating case (
|a|\ll M
) in
[
DR11
,
DR13
,
DR10a
,
TT08
,
AB15
]
.

In the present paper, we will apply microlocal energy currents as in
[
DR11
,
DRSR16
]
to deal with
(\omega,\ell)
outside of a compact set; see Step 1 of §
1.2
.

As mentioned above, energy
boundedness
estimates and
integrated
local energy estimates are coupled in the proof of
[
DRSR16
]
, due to the presence of superradiance in Kerr. In
[
SRT25
]
it was, however, shown that energy boundedness estimates can be obtained independently from integrated local energy estimates, when restricting to solutions supported on high, trapped (but non-superradiant) frequencies.

Finally, we note that in the case of (massive) wave equations on sub-extremal Reissner–Nordström–de Sitter spacetimes, integrated energy estimates were obtained in
[
Veg24
]
.

1.1.2.
Integrated energy estimates on extremal black holes

The first integrated energy estimates for the wave equation on extremal black hole spacetimes were obtained on extremal Reissner–Nordström in
[
Are11
]
, employing similar vector fields multipliers to
[
DR09
]
, exploiting the presence of a photon sphere at
\{r=2M\}
and the existence of a degenerate energy boundedness estimate that is independent of local integrated energy estimates. In
[
Are11
]
, a preliminary analogue of the
r^{p}
-weighted energy estimates was introduced near the event horizon, which was later fully developed in
[
AAG20
]
.

Integrated energy estimates for the wave equation on extremal Kerr were established first for axisymmetric solutions (
m=0
) in
[
Are12
]
, see also
[
GW24b
]
, and for solutions supported on a finite number of azimuthal modes (
m\neq 0
) in
[
Gaj23a
]
, the latter assuming future-integrability. As the results in
[
Gaj23a
]
are closely connected to results of the present paper, we provide a more detailed comparison in §
1.3
.

1.1.3.
Integrated estimates for charged scalar fields

When viewing the Minkowski spacetime as a solution to the Einstein–Maxwell equations, the corresponding Faraday tensor
F
vanishes. The linearization of the Maxwell–charged scalar field equations around
(\phi,F)=(0,0)
results in the standard wave equation on Minkowski.
6
6

6

One can nevertheless study the charged scalar field equation on the Minkowski background around a non-zero
F
, namely
F=-Qr^{-2}dt\wedge dr
corresponding to the Reissner–Nordström solution, see also
[
GVdM24
]
. This equation plays a fundamental role in the precise late-time asymptotics derived in
[
Gaj26
]
.
The nonlinear Maxwell–charged scalar field system, however, does feature a non-zero
F
, which has as significant long-range effect on the analysis. For this system, there is a large body of literature ranging from global well-posedness results for large initial data, see
[
EM82a
,
EM82b
,
KM94
,
Yan18
]
, to decay estimates for small initial data
[
LS06
,
Yan16
]
. In particular,
[
Yan16
,
Yan18
]
feature integrated local energy decay estimates and
r^{p}
-weighted energy estimates.

A key additional difficulty that arises when considering charged scalar fields on black hole backgrounds is the non-decay of
F
in time, due to the fact that
F
is non-decaying in the absence of a scalar field.

This difficulty affects both integrated local energy decay estimates and
r^{p}
-weighted energy estimates.
It was first addressed in the context of the Maxwell–charged scalar field system in spherical symmetry with small scalar field charge parameters
\mathfrak{q}
on sub-extremal Reissner–Nordström backgrounds in
[
VdM22
]
. In particular, integrated local energy estimates and
r^{p}
-weighted energy estimates were established, exploiting both spherical symmetry and smallness of
\mathfrak{q}
.

Let us note that in the context of the Maxwell–charged scalar fields (on both Minkowski and black hole backgrounds), there exists a conserved, non-negative definite (degenerate) energy for the pair
(\phi,F)
. In contrast, for the linearized equation (
1.2
) there exists no such non-negative definite conserved quantity, allowing in principle for solutions that are growing in time.
7
7

7

Note that the existence of solutions that grow in time for the linearized system can in principle be compatible with a bounded energy for the nonlinear system, even for small initial data, since mere boundedness of the energy in the nonlinear system does not need to guarantee (sufficiently rapid) convergence to the zero solution, and therefore decay of nonlinear terms.

Finally, we note that the wave equation with an asymptotically inverse-square potential serves as an important toy model that captures some of the difficulties that appear in the case of large scalar field charge coupling constant
\mathfrak{q}
. In this case, integrated energy estimates (local and
r^{p}
-weighted) were obtained in
[
Gaj23b
]
.

1.2.
Sketch of the proof

The proof of Theorem
1.1
can be divided into four main steps:

Step 1
:

Derive weighted
L^{2}
-estimates for the Fourier transform of
\phi
in
t
and on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, restricted to high frequencies, assuming a priori that
\phi
is
future integrable
in time to make sense of Fourier-transformed expressions.

Step 2
:

Derive weighted
L^{2}
-estimates for the Fourier transform, restricted to bounded frequencies.

Step 3
:

Apply Plancherel’s theorem to obtain weighted
L^{2}
-estimates for
\phi
(under the assumption of future integrability).

Step 4
:

Verify the assumption of future-integrability for
\kappa_{+}>0
and
\kappa_{c}>0
and then take the limit
\kappa_{c},\kappa_{+}\downarrow 0
.

1.2.1.
Fourier transform

Let
(t,r,\theta,\varphi)
denote the standard coordinates on Reissner–Nordström(–de Sitter) with
\Lambda\geq 0
. Consider the electromagnetic gauge:

A=-\frac{Q}{r}dt.

Let
\psi=r\phi
. In order to Fourier transform in
t
, we first extend
\psi
as a function on the full manifold and then restrict:

\uppsi(t,r,\theta,\varphi):=\xi(\tau(t,r))\cdot\psi(t,r,\theta,\varphi),

where
\xi:\mathbb{R}_{\tau}\to[0,1]
is a smooth cut-off function satisfying
\xi(\tau)=0
for
\tau\leq 0
and
\xi(\tau)=1
for
\tau\geq 1
.

Note that then

(g^{-1}_{M,Q,\Lambda})^{\mu\nu}\>(^{A}D)_{\mu}\>(^{A}D)_{\nu}(r^{-1}\uppsi)-\frac{2\Lambda}{3}(r^{-1}\uppsi)=\xi\cdot G_{A}+F_{\xi},

where
F_{\xi}
is compactly supported in
0\leq\tau\leq 1
.

Assuming that
\uppsi(t,\cdot)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{t})
, the
t
-Fourier transform
\mathfrak{F}(\uppsi)(\omega,\cdot)
is well-defined. We perform a Fourier transform on
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\theta,\varphi}
by decomposing into spherical harmonics:

u_{\ell m}(r_{*};\omega):=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathfrak{F}(\uppsi)(\omega,r(r_{*}),\theta,\varphi)\overline{Y}_{\ell m}(\theta,\varphi)\,\sin\theta d\theta d\varphi,

where
r_{*}
is a tortoise coordinate, satisfying
\frac{dr_{*}}{dr}=\Omega^{-2}
, and
Y_{\ell m}
denote spherical harmonics.
8
8

8

Note that since Reissner–Nordström spacetimes are spherically symmetric, the Fourier transforms in
t
and
\mathbb{S}^{2}
commute.

Then
u_{\ell m}
satisfies a Schrödinger equation:

u_{\ell m}^{\prime\prime}+\left(\omega^{2}-V_{\ell\omega}\right)u_{\ell m}=H_{\ell m},

(1.4)

with
H_{\ell m}(r_{*},\omega)
the Fourier transform of
r\Omega^{2}(F_{\xi}+\xi G_{A})_{\ell m}(t,r_{*})
and
V_{\ell\omega}
functions of
r_{*}
.

It is convenient to consider the following dimensionless charge parameter:

q:=\mathfrak{q}Q.

It will also be more convenient to introduce the following shifted frequencies:

\displaystyle\widehat{\omega}:=\omega-qr_{c}^{-1},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\omega}:=\omega-qr_{+}^{-1},

with
r_{+}
the event horizon radius and
r_{c}
the cosmological horizon radius (
r_{c}=\infty
when
\Lambda=0
)) as these show up in the following boundary conditions for
u_{\ell m}
as
r_{*}\to\pm\infty
(which should be interpreted in an
L^{2}_{\omega}
-sense):

\displaystyle u_{\ell m}(r_{*};\omega)\sim

\displaystyle\>e^{-i\widetilde{\omega}r_{*}-iq\int_{0}^{r_{*}}\rho_{+}(r(r_{*}^{\prime}))\,dr_{*}^{\prime}}\quad(r_{*}\to-\infty),

\displaystyle u_{\ell m}(r_{*};\omega)\sim

\displaystyle\>e^{+i\widehat{\omega}r_{*}-iq\int_{0}^{r_{*}}\rho_{c}(r(r_{*}^{\prime}))\,dr_{*}^{\prime}}\quad(r_{*}\to\infty),

where

\displaystyle\rho_{+}:=

\displaystyle\>r_{+}^{-1}-r^{-1},

\displaystyle\rho_{c}:=

\displaystyle\>r^{-1}-r_{c}^{-1}.

Then we define the shifted potentials
\widehat{V}_{\ell\omega}
and
\widetilde{V}_{\ell\omega}
as follows:

\omega^{2}-V_{\ell\omega}=:\widehat{\omega}^{2}-\widehat{V}_{\ell\widehat{\omega}}=:\widetilde{\omega}^{2}-\widetilde{V}_{\ell\widetilde{\omega}}.

Since
\omega^{2}-V_{\ell\omega}
does not play an important role, we will only consider
\widehat{\omega}
and
\widetilde{\omega}
and redefine:
V_{\ell\widehat{\omega}}=\widehat{V}_{\ell\widehat{\omega}}
.

Then:

V_{\ell\widehat{\omega}}(r)=\ell(\ell+1)\Omega^{2}r^{-2}-{q}^{2}\rho_{c}^{2}(r)+2{q}\widehat{\omega}\rho_{c}(r)+\Omega^{2}\left[r^{-1}\frac{d\Omega^{2}}{dr}+\frac{2\Lambda}{3}\right].

The goal at the Fourier-transformed level is to derive appropriate uniform
L^{2}_{\omega}
-estimates, so that we can then apply Plancherel’s theorem to arrive at integrated-in-time energy estimates in physical space.

1.2.2.
Step 1: high-frequency regimes

We partition the high-frequency regime as follows:

•

\widehat{\omega}
-dominated:
|\widehat{\omega}|\gg\ell+1
,

•

\ell
-dominated:
\ell\gg|\widehat{\omega}|+1
,

•

trapped:
|\widehat{\omega}|\sim\ell\gg 1
.

In each of these frequency regimes, we derive weighted
L^{2}_{r_{*}}
-estimates via the consideration of
multipliers
of the form (where we omit the subscripts
m\ell
):

\textnormal{Re}\,(hu\cdot\overline{H}),\quad\textnormal{Re}\,(yu^{\prime}\cdot\overline{H}),\quad\textnormal{Re}\,(f^{\prime}u\overline{H}+2fu^{\prime}\overline{H}),\quad\textnormal{Re}\,(i\widehat{\omega}u\cdot\overline{H}),\quad\textnormal{Re}\,(i\widetilde{\omega}u\cdot\overline{H}),

with
h,y,f:\mathbb{R}_{r_{*}}\to\mathbb{R}
, as well as appropriate linear combinations, and then integrating by parts.

In addition to the above multipliers, we need to consider multipliers corresponding to
(\Omega^{-1}r)^{p}
-estimates in frequency space:

\textnormal{Re}\,((\Omega^{-1}r)^{p}w^{\prime}e^{i\widehat{\omega}r_{*}-iq\int_{0}^{r_{*}}\rho_{c}(r_{*}^{\prime})\,dr_{*}^{\prime}}\overline{H}),\quad\textnormal{Re}\,((\Omega^{-1}r)^{p}v^{\prime}e^{-i\widetilde{\omega}r_{*}-iq\int_{0}^{r_{*}}\rho_{+}(r_{*}^{\prime})\,dr_{*}^{\prime}}\overline{H}),

where

v=e^{i\widetilde{\omega}r_{*}+iq\int_{0}^{r_{*}}\rho_{+}(r_{*}^{\prime})\,dr_{*}^{\prime}}u,\quad w=e^{-i\widehat{\omega}r_{*}+iq\int_{0}^{r_{*}}\rho_{c}(r_{*}^{\prime})\,dr_{*}^{\prime}}u.

In particular, the multipliers
\textnormal{Re}\,(i\widehat{\omega}u\cdot\overline{H})
and
\textnormal{Re}\,(i\widetilde{\omega}u\cdot\overline{H})
may be interpreted as the Fourier-transformed analogues of energy estimates with respect to the multipliers
K_{+}\psi
and
K_{c}\psi
, respectively, where

K_{+}=T+iqr_{+}^{-1}
and
K_{c}=T+iqr_{c}^{-1}
(and
K_{c}=T
if
\Lambda=0
). Integrating by parts results in:

\displaystyle\widehat{\omega}^{2}|u|^{2}(\infty)+\widehat{\omega}\widetilde{\omega}|u|^{2}(-\infty)=

\displaystyle\>\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widehat{\omega}\textnormal{Re}\,(iu\overline{H}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\omega}^{2}|u|^{2}(-\infty)+\widehat{\omega}\widetilde{\omega}|u|^{2}(\infty)=

\displaystyle\>\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widetilde{\omega}\textnormal{Re}\,(iu\overline{H}).

Note that the two terms on the left-hand side are each non-negative only if:

\widehat{\omega}\widetilde{\omega}\geq 0.

We then say
\omega
is
non-superradiant
. Superradiant frequencies correspond to
\widehat{\omega}\widetilde{\omega}<0
, or equivalently:

\widehat{\omega}(\widehat{\omega}-q\rho_{+}(r_{c}))<0,

which corresponds to
q\widehat{\omega}\in(0,q^{2}\rho_{+}(r_{c}))
.

Superradiance forms an obstruction, as it prevents us from controlling boundary terms at
r_{*}=\pm\infty
using the Fourier transform of
K_{+}
-energy estimates and
K_{c}
-energy estimates.

An important property of
V_{\ell\widehat{\omega}}
is that in the superradiant frequency regime,
\widehat{\omega}\widetilde{\omega}<0
,

V_{\ell\widehat{\omega}}(r_{\rm max})-\widehat{\omega}^{2}>0.

We say that
superradiant frequencies are not trapped
. This nomenclature is be motivated by the dynamics of free-falling, charged, massless particles. More precisely, we consider massive particles with charge
e
and mass
m
, restricted to the equatorial plane, satisfying the (spacetime version) of the Lorentz force law and then take the limit
m\downarrow 0
,
e\to 0
, such that
\frac{e}{m}\to\mathfrak{q}\neq 0
. The effective potential
V_{\rm eff}
appearing in the corresponding equations of motion then satisfies:

V_{\rm eff}(r)=L^{2}\Omega^{2}r^{-2}-{q}^{2}\rho_{c}^{2}(r)+2{q}\hat{E}\rho_{c}(r)+q^{2}r_{c}^{-2},

where
L
is the magnitude of the angular momentum and
\hat{E}:=p_{t}+qr_{c}^{-1}
, with
p_{t}
the canonical momentum associated to time translations. The pair
(\hat{E},L)
are conserved along the motion of the particle.

Note that, up to a frequency-independent and charge-independent term,
V_{\rm eff}(r)
agrees with
V_{\widehat{\omega}\ell}
under the identification
\widehat{\omega}\mapsto\hat{E}
and
L^{2}\mapsto\ell(\ell+1)
. The charged particle is trapped at
r=r_{\rm max}
if
V_{\rm eff}(r_{\rm\max})-\hat{E}^{2}=0
.

Superradiant frequencies correspond to charged particles with
q\hat{E}\in(0,q^{2}\rho_{+}(r_{c}))
. It can be shown that in this case,
V_{\rm eff}(r_{\rm max})-\hat{E}^{2}>0
, so these particles are not trapped.

Since superradiant frequencies
\widehat{\omega}
are bounded, they only affect the
\ell
-dominated frequencies
|\widehat{\omega}|\ll\ell+1
of our high-frequency regimes.

The form of the potential
V_{\ell\omega}
when
\ell\gg|\widehat{\omega}|+1
is sketched in Figure
1
below.

Figure 1
.
A sketch of the graph of the potential
V_{\ell\omega}
as a function of
\rho_{+}
in the high,
\ell
-dominated frequency regime with
q\widetilde{\omega}<0
.

We make the following observations:

•

the maximum
r_{\rm max}
is located very close to
r_{\sharp}
, the
Reissner–Nordström(-de Sitter) photon sphere radius
and

V_{\ell\widehat{\omega}}(r_{\rm max})-\widehat{\omega}^{2}\gtrsim\ell(\ell+1).

•

In the non-superradiant case with
q\widetilde{\omega}<0
and
q\widehat{\omega}<0
,
V_{\ell\widehat{\omega}}
has a minimum to the right of the maximum for suitably small
\kappa_{c}
. As
\frac{q\widehat{\omega}}{\ell(\ell+1)}\uparrow 0
, the minimum approaches
\rho_{+}=r_{+}^{-1}-r_{c}^{-1}
. Analogously, it can be shown that when
\kappa_{+}
is suitably small and
q\widetilde{\omega}>0
and
q\widetilde{\omega}>0
,
V
has a minimum to the left of the maximum, which approaches
\rho_{+}=0
as
q\widetilde{\omega}\downarrow 0
. Finally, when
q\widehat{\omega}\cdot q\widetilde{\omega}<0
(the superradiant regime),
V_{\ell\widehat{\omega}}
has a single extremum, a maximum.

The first observation provides a quantitative version of the statement that superradiant frequencies are not trapped.

The second observation concerns the main difficulty in the high-frequency analysis and may be thought of as the main characteristic feature of the absence of red-shift and the presence of a scalar field charge in the high-frequency regime.

In the
q\widetilde{\omega},q\widehat{\omega}<0
case, we resolve this difficulty by exploiting the fact that the minimum occurs for large values of
r
(
\rho_{c}
close to zero), where we can consider the Fourier versions of
(\Omega^{-1}r)^{p}
-weighted energy estimates above. The same strategy works in the
q\widetilde{\omega},q\widehat{\omega}>0
case. While such weighted energy estimates do not hold in general in physical space, we can exploit the fact that
\ell\gg|\widehat{\omega}|
to ensure their validity in this frequency regime. It is important to emphasize here that we do
not
make use of the red-shift effect near the event or cosmological horizons, as this would result in estimates that are not uniform as
\kappa_{c}\downarrow 0
or
\kappa_{+}\downarrow 0
.

In the superradiant case (
q\widetilde{\omega}\cdot q\widehat{\omega}<0
), the potential has a single extremum, so the above difficulty does not occur. However, in that case boundary terms at
r=r_{+}
and
r=r_{+}
(or
r=\infty
) do not have a good sign, so we cannot use the Fourier versions of
K_{+}
-energy estimates and
K_{c}
-energy estimates and we instead exploit, as in
[
DRSR16
]
, the largeness of
V_{\ell\widehat{\omega}}(r_{\rm max})-\widehat{\omega}^{2}\gtrsim\ell(\ell+1)
to absorb these boundary terms by employing (Fourier versions of)
K_{+}
-energy estimates and
K_{c}
-energy estimates with appropriate cut-off functions.

The trapped frequencies can be treated analogously. Since these frequencies are non-superradiant, the main remaining difficulty is the presence of a minimum of the potential near
\rho_{+}=0
or
\rho_{c}=0
, as above, which necessitates a coupling with
(\Omega^{-1}r)^{p}
-weighted estimates as above. The main difference is the appearance of a degenerate factor near
r=r_{\sharp}
due to the fact that
V_{\ell\widehat{\omega}}(r_{\rm max})
or
\widehat{\omega}^{2}-V_{\ell\widehat{\omega}}(r_{\rm max})
cannot be bounded below.

We finally note that the large
\widehat{\omega}
-frequency regime is largely independent of the precise behaviour of the potential and proceeds in a straightforward manner. In particular, we do not need to appeal to
(\Omega^{-1}r)^{p}
weighted energy estimates (which would would
a priori
also not be valid in this frequency regime!).

The conclusion of Step 1 can be stated very schematically as the following inequality, where we ignore all (important!)
r
-weights on the left-hand side:

c\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}_{r_{*}}}|u^{\prime}|^{2}+\widehat{\omega}^{2}|u|^{2}+|u|^{2}\,dr_{*}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}_{r_{*}}}\textnormal{Re}\,(yu^{\prime}\cdot\overline{H})+\textnormal{Re}\,(hu\cdot\overline{H})+\textnormal{Re}\,(f^{\prime}u\overline{H}+2fu^{\prime}\overline{H})+\textnormal{Re}\,(i\widehat{\omega}u\cdot\overline{H})+\textnormal{Re}\,(i\widetilde{\omega}u\cdot\overline{H})\,dr_{*}\\
-\int_{\mathbb{R}_{r_{*}}}\textnormal{Re}\,((\Omega^{-1}r)^{p}w^{\prime}e^{i\widehat{\omega}r_{*}-iq\int_{0}^{r_{*}}\rho_{c}(r_{*}^{\prime})\,dr_{*}^{\prime}}\overline{H})+\textnormal{Re}\,((\Omega^{-1}r)^{p}v^{\prime}e^{-i\widetilde{\omega}r_{*}-iq\int_{0}^{r_{*}}\rho_{+}(r_{*}^{\prime})\,dr_{*}^{\prime}}\overline{H})\,dr_{*},

(1.5)

where
h,y,f
denote functions that arise from the multipliers described above and may have some
\omega
dependence in a tractable way, but not to leading-order near
r=r_{+}
and
r=r_{c}
. The constant
c>0
, importantly, does not depend on
\omega
or
\ell
.

1.2.3.
Step 2: bounded frequency regimes

In the case of bounded frequencies, we can (formally) apply Green’s formula to express:

u(r_{*})=\frac{u_{\infty}(r_{*})}{\mathfrak{W}}\int_{-\infty}^{r_{*}}u_{+}(r_{*}^{\prime})H(r_{*}^{\prime})\,dr_{*}^{\prime}+\frac{u_{+}(r_{*})}{\mathfrak{W}}\int_{r_{*}}^{\infty}u_{\infty}(r_{*}^{\prime})H(r_{*}^{\prime})\,dr_{*}^{\prime},

(1.6)

with
u_{\infty}
and
u_{+}
solutions to (
1.4
) with
H=0
, satisfying:

\displaystyle u_{+}\sim

\displaystyle\>e^{-i\widetilde{\omega}r_{*}-iq\int_{0}^{r_{*}}\rho_{+}(r(r_{*}^{\prime}))\,dr_{*}^{\prime}}\quad(r_{*}\to-\infty),

\displaystyle u_{\infty}(r_{*})\sim

\displaystyle\>e^{+i\widehat{\omega}r_{*}-iq\int_{0}^{r_{*}}\rho_{c}(r(r_{*}^{\prime}))\,dr_{*}^{\prime}}\quad(r_{*}\to\infty),

and with
\mathfrak{W}=\mathfrak{W}(\omega,\ell)
the corresponding Wronskian. The goal is to obtain suitable estimates for
u_{+}
,
u_{\infty}
and
\mathfrak{W}
and then convert these into weighted
L^{2}
-estimates via Green’s formula. This problem can be divided into two parts:

(1)

An analysis
away from

\widehat{\omega}=0
and
\widetilde{\omega}=0
, where
u_{+}
and
u_{\infty}
can easily be controlled and boundedness of
|\mathfrak{W}|^{-1}
may be thought of as a consequence of the fact that
\mathfrak{W}\neq 0
together with continuity of
\mathfrak{W}
along the real axis away from
\widehat{\omega}=0
and
\widetilde{\omega}=0
. The statement
\mathfrak{W}\neq 0
is known as
mode-stability on the real axis
, because
\mathfrak{W}(\omega,\ell)=0
would imply the existence of finite energy solutions
\psi_{\ell m}(t,r)=e^{-i\omega t}u(r)
which are non-decaying.

(2)

An analysis
near

\widehat{\omega}=0
and
\widetilde{\omega}=0
. As
\widehat{\omega}\to 0
,
r_{*}=\infty
changes from a regular singular point of (
1.4
) to an irregular singular point in the case
\kappa_{c}=0
. The same change occurs as
\widetilde{\omega}\to 0
for
r_{*}=-\infty
in the case
\kappa_{+}=0
.

We first establish mode stability on the real axis in the case
\kappa_{c}=\kappa_{+}=0
by applying the methods developed in
[
TdC20
]
and then extend this to the near-extremal case
\kappa_{c}\ll\kappa_{+}\ll 1
.
This is the only part of the analysis where appeal to (near-)extremality
1-\frac{|Q|}{M}\ll 1
!

The rest of the analysis concerns small frequencies:
|\widehat{\omega}|\ll 1
or
|\widetilde{\omega}|\ll 1
. This part contains the main novel aspects of the analysis in the present paper, which includes a mathematically rigorous realization of the small-frequency “matched asymptotics” method that is frequently applied in the black hole setting in the physics literature, see for example the foundational works
[
Sta73
,
SC74
,
TP74
]
as well as
[
HP98
,
GZ18
]
in the charged scalar field on (extremal) Reissner–Nordström. In contrast with the above works, we need to provide rigorous estimates for relevant error terms to be able to conclude quantitative estimates.

Consider solutions
u
(
1.4
) with
H=0
. We can rewrite the ODE in two different ways:

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}U_{+}}{ds_{+}^{2}}=

\displaystyle\>\rho_{+}^{4}(r^{-2}\Omega^{2})^{-2}\mathcal{V}U_{+},

(1.7)

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}U_{c}}{ds_{c}^{2}}=

\displaystyle\>\rho_{c}^{4}(r^{-2}\Omega^{2})^{-2}\mathcal{V}U_{c},

(1.8)

where
s_{+}=\frac{1}{\rho_{+}}-r_{+}
,
s_{c}=\frac{1}{\rho_{c}}-r_{+}
,
U_{+}(s_{+})=(s_{+}+r_{+})(r^{-1}\Omega u)(r_{*}(s_{+}))
,
U_{c}(s_{c})=(s_{c}+r_{+})(r^{-1}\Omega u)(r_{*}(s_{c}))
and:

\mathcal{V}_{\widehat{\omega}\ell}(r)=-\widehat{\omega}^{2}+V_{\widehat{\omega}\ell}(r)+\Omega^{3}\frac{d^{2}\Omega}{dr^{2}}(r).

The coordinates
s_{+}
and
s_{c}
are natural for treating the cases
\kappa_{c}\geq 0
and
\kappa_{+}\geq 0
in a uniform manner.

The key idea is to approximate the solutions
U_{+}
and
U_{c}
by appropriate special functions in different regions and establish the existence of an overlap region, where the approximations coincide.

For simplicity, in the discussion below, we will restrict to the extremal case
\kappa_{c}=\kappa_{+}=0
(so
\widehat{\omega}=\omega
) and we will set the length scale to
r_{+}=1
. Denote:

\beta_{\ell}=\sqrt{(2\ell+1)^{2}-4q^{2}}.

In that case, we can split the
r
-domain
[1,\infty)
as follows:

•

Region I:
When

\frac{1}{2}|\omega|^{\frac{1}{4}}|\widetilde{\omega}|^{-\frac{1}{4}}\leq s_{+}<\infty,

we can approximate:

U_{+}\approx B_{1}W_{-i\sigma,\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\ell}}(-2i\widetilde{\omega}s_{+})+B_{2}M_{-i\sigma,\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\ell}}(-2i\widetilde{\omega}s_{+}),

where
W_{\varkappa,\mu},M_{\varkappa,\mu}
are standard Whittaker functions and
\sigma=-q+2\widetilde{\omega}
. Note that in the limit
\omega\to 0
,
U_{+}
becomes
exactly
a linear combination of Whittaker functions.

•

Region II:
When

\frac{1}{2}|\widetilde{\omega}|^{\frac{1}{4}}|\omega|^{-\frac{1}{4}}\leq s_{c}<\infty,

we can approximate:

U_{c}\approx D_{1}W_{-i\sigma,\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\ell}}(-2i\omega s_{c})+D_{2}M_{-i\sigma,\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\ell}}(-2i\omega s_{c}),

with now
\sigma=-q-2\widetilde{\omega}
. Note that in the limit
\widetilde{\omega}\to 0
,
U_{c}
becomes
exactly
a linear combination of Whittaker functions.

Since
s_{c}=r-1
and
s_{+}=\frac{1}{r-1}
, we can equivalently characterize Region II as follows:

0<s_{+}\leq 2|\omega|^{\frac{1}{4}}|\widetilde{\omega}|^{-\frac{1}{4}},

so Regions I and II overlap and their intersection corresponds to:
\frac{1}{2}|\omega|^{\frac{1}{4}}|\widetilde{\omega}|^{-\frac{1}{4}}\leq s_{+}\leq 2|\omega|^{\frac{1}{4}}|\widetilde{\omega}|^{-\frac{1}{4}}
. Furthermore,
U_{c}=s_{+}^{-1}U_{+}
.

Consider now the case of small
|\widetilde{\omega}|
. Setting
u=u_{+}
fixes the constants
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
. In particular,
B_{2}=0
. Since both approximations are valid in the overlap region
and
the arguments in all the Whittaker functions are small, we can determine the constants
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
from the small
z
-asymptotics of
W_{\varkappa,\mu}(z),M_{\varkappa,\mu}(z)
. Furthermore,
|\mathfrak{W}|^{-1}\sim|\widetilde{\omega}|^{-1}D_{2}^{-1}
, which provides a bound for the Wronskian:

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{W}|^{-1}\sim

\displaystyle\>|\widetilde{\omega}|^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\textnormal{Re}\,\beta_{\ell}}\quad(\beta_{\ell}\neq 0),

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{W}|^{-1}\sim

\displaystyle\>|\widetilde{\omega}|^{-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{1}{1+\log((1+|\widetilde{\omega}|)^{-1})}\quad(\beta_{\ell}=0),

where the logarithmic term arises from the logarithm appearing in the small
z
-asymptotics of the Whittaker function
M_{\varkappa,\mu}(z)
when
\mu=0
. Furthermore, the estimates on
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
also result in global, uniform estimates for
u_{+}
.

Choosing
u=u_{\infty}
instead fixes
D_{1}
and also fixes
D_{2}=0
. Then estimates for
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
follow, which therefore also give global, uniform estimates for
u_{\infty}
. The small-
|\omega|
case proceeds entirely analogously.

The above argument relies on precise properties of Whittaker functions, such as explicit connection formulas between
W_{\varkappa,\mu}(z)
and
M_{\varkappa,\mu}(z)
!

While an approximation by Whittaker functions is sufficient for the extremal case, it is insufficient for deriving uniform estimates in
\kappa_{+}
and
\kappa_{c}
in the near-extremal case, which are required for our analysis. In this case, we need to split the
r
-domain into four regions in total. When
|\widetilde{\omega}|\gg\kappa_{+}
and
|\widehat{\omega}|\gg\kappa_{c}
, we can still use Whittaker functions away from the event horizon and cosmological horizon/null infinity. Near the horizons, we can instead apply a standard WKB or Liouville–Green approximation and approximate the solutions by oscillating exponentials.

When
|\widetilde{\omega}|\lesssim\kappa_{+}
or
|\widehat{\omega}|\lesssim\kappa_{c}
, we instead approximate
U_{+}
and
U_{c}
by
Gauss hypergeometric functions
. These can then be matched to other hypergeometric or Whittaker functions, as above. Note that the small-
|\widetilde{\omega}|
analysis requires smallness of
\kappa_{+}
and
\kappa_{c}
, whereas the small-
|\widehat{\omega}|
analysis requires only smallness of
\kappa_{c}
.

We conclude the following uniform estimates for
\mathfrak{W}
, valid in the full bounded frequency regime (under the assumption of mode stability when
\kappa_{+}
is not small):

|\mathfrak{W}|^{-1}\sim(|\widetilde{\omega}|+\kappa_{+})^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\textnormal{Re}\,\beta_{\ell}}(|\widehat{\omega}|+\kappa_{c})^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\textnormal{Re}\,\beta_{\ell}}\frac{1}{1+\delta_{\beta_{\ell}0}\log((1+|\widetilde{\omega}|+\kappa_{+})^{-1}(1+|\widehat{\omega}|+\kappa_{c})^{-1})}.

In the remainder of the bounded-frequency analysis, we apply (
7.50
) to conclude the following schematic inequality, where we ignore (important!)
r
-dependent weights on both sides:

c\cdot\int_{|\omega|\lesssim 1}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{r_{*}}}(\ldots)|u|^{2}\,dr_{*}d\omega\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}(\ldots)|F_{\xi}|^{2}+(\ldots)|G_{A}|^{2}\,\sin\theta d\theta d\varphi drd\tau.

(1.9)

Note that in contrast with (
1.5
), (
1.9
) already has physical-space norms on the right-hand side, which is necessary in order to obtain
L^{2}
-estimates via (
7.50
) with the desired
r
-weights; see also
[
SR15
]
[§3.1] for a related analysis.

Let us finally mention that a precise analysis of analogues of
u_{+}
and
u_{\infty}
also plays an important role in the late-time analysis of the massive Klein–Gordon equation on Schwarzschild in
[
PSRVdM23
,
SRVdM26
]
. In that case, the critical frequencies
\omega=\pm m
, with
m
the Klein–Gordon mass, play an analogous role to
\omega=0
and
\omega=\mathfrak{q}Qr_{+}^{-1}
in the present paper. For
|\omega|<m
, homogeneous solutions are approximated by matching an approximation by Bessel functions, to a WKB/turning point approximation (in a large-
r
region). This stands in contrast with earlier work from the physics literature (in the bounded angular frequency regime), see for example
[
KT02
]
, where Whittaker functions are used (for bounded angular frequencies) at suitably large values of
r
and are matched to hypergeometric functions which cover the rest of the region (and describe exactly the behaviour of solutions at
\omega=\pm m
).

In the Klein–Gordon setting, the turning points of the relevant potentials occur at large absolute values of the relevant rescaled argument
x
appearing in the Whittaker function
W_{\varkappa,\mu}(x)
in
[
KT02
]
(with
|x|\sim\frac{r}{\sqrt{m^{2}-\omega^{2}}}
). Note that for large
|x|
, the Whittaker functions themselves can be approximated via a WKB/turning point analysis. Furthermore,
\varkappa
scales like
\frac{1}{\sqrt{m^{2}-\omega^{2}}}
, which implies that for
|x|\ll\varkappa
, the Whittaker functions can instead be approximated by Bessel functions. This is what allows
[
PSRVdM23
]
to circumvent the use of Whittaker functions altogether in the analysis.

In the charged scalar field setting, there are two key differences: 1) the turning points of the potential
V
do
not
occur at large (or small) values of the relevant rescaled variables
|\widetilde{\omega}s_{+}|
and
|\widehat{\omega}s_{c}|
of the Whittaker functions, so the Whittaker functions
cannot
be approximated via a WKB/turning point analysis in the relevant regions, and 2) the Whittaker parameter
\varkappa
is bounded, so Bessel functions also do not form adequate approximations. For this reason, Whittaker/hypergeometric functions play an essential role in the analysis of the present paper. It would nevertheless be interesting to revisit the setting of
[
PSRVdM23
]
with a matched asymptotics argument centered around Whittaker and hypergeometric functions, as in the present paper.

We refer to the upcoming work
[
TdC
]
for a related matched-asymptotics analysis in the setting of more general Heun equations.

1.2.4.
Step 3: Plancherel

Combining Steps 1 and 2 amounts to summing up the inequalities (
1.5
) and (
1.9
). We can freely apply Plancherel to the left-hand side of the summed estimate, but since the right-hand side has non-trivial frequency dependence, applying Plancherel to the right-hand side is less straightforward.

The resolution to this difficulty is the observation that the functions in the multipliers appearing in Step 1 can be chosen in such a way that they agree for all frequencies near the horizons up to higher order terms in
\rho_{+}
or
\rho_{c}
, which can be dealt with by applying Young’s inequality and absorbing the arising
u
-dependent terms into the left-hand side. In particular, this means that we require also multiplier estimates for the bounded frequencies of Step 2, but since we can use the estimate (
1.9
), they simplify greatly.

After writing the estimates at the level of
u
in a sufficiently uniform way, Plancherel can be obtained to derive the desired physical space integrated energy estimates.

1.2.5.
Step 4: verifying future integrability

Since the final integrated energy estimates of Step 3 are only valid under the assumption of sufficient integrability of
\uppsi
and
\xi G
in
\tau
, which follows from
future integrability
(in
\tau\in[0,\infty)
) of
\psi
(and
G_{A}
), it remains to verify this assumption on
\psi
. Note that the assumption is
qualitative
in nature, in contrast with the conclusion of Step 3, which was a
quantitative
statement. In particular, we can restrict to fixed angular frequencies
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
without loss of generality.

The idea is to use both
\kappa_{+}
and
\kappa_{c}
as
regularizers
. That is to say, we can first consider the near-extremal Reissner–Nordström–de Sitter case, where
\kappa_{+}>0
and
\kappa_{c}>0
and derive future integrability, using crucially the strict positivity of the surface gravities. Then the quantitative estimates in Step 3, which are uniform
\kappa_{+}
and
\kappa_{c}
, remain valid as
\kappa_{+}\downarrow 0
and
\kappa_{c}\downarrow 0
, allowing us to conclude the validity of the estimates (and as a consequence, future integrability) in the
\kappa_{c}=\kappa_{+}=0
case.

We derive future integrability in the
0<\kappa_{c}<\kappa_{+}
case via a continuity argument in the charge parameter
q
(keeping
Q
fixed). The starting point is future integrability for small
|q|
; see
[
Bes20
]
[Theorem 4.2]. Alternatively, we could appeal to the integrated energy estimates for
q=0

[
Veg24
]
[Proposition 11] from which future integrability for small
|q|
follows straightforwardly.

Assuming future integrability for
q=q_{0}
, we consider nearby
q_{1}
and the time-dependent charge parameter
q_{\tau}=\xi_{\tau}q_{1}+(1-\xi_{\tau})q_{0}
, where
\xi_{\tau}(\tau^{\prime})
is a smooth cut-off function that vanishes for
\tau^{\prime}\geq\tau
and equal to 1 for
\tau^{\prime}\leq\tau-\delta
with
\delta>0
. We then treat the terms involving
q_{\tau}-q_{0}
as an inhomogeneity and apply integrated estimates for
q=q_{0}
. For sufficiently small
|q_{1}-q_{0}|
, this inhomogeneity can be absorbed into the left-hand side of the estimate, if we make use of the fact that
\kappa_{c}>0
and
\kappa_{+}>0
. That is to say, we make use of
red-shift estimates
near the horizon. This forms the key step towards extending the range of
q
for which future integrability holds.

1.3.
Comparison with wave equation on extremal Kerr

After Fourier transforming in time and decomposing into spheroidal harmonic modes, integrated energy estimates in extremal Kerr are determined by an analysis at the level of an inhomogeneous radial ODE:

u^{\prime\prime}+(\omega^{2}-V_{m\ell\omega})u=H.

We fix
|a|=M
and
r_{+}=1
and define
\widetilde{\omega}^{2}-\widetilde{V}=\omega^{2}-V_{m\ell\omega}
with
\widetilde{\omega}=\omega-\frac{m}{2}
. Then we obtain the following asymptotic behaviour around
r=1
:

\widetilde{V}_{m\ell\omega}(r(r_{*}))=2am\widetilde{\omega}r_{*}^{-1}+\left[\Lambda_{m\ell}\left(\frac{m}{2}\right)-2m^{2}+O(m\widetilde{\omega})\right]r_{*}^{-2}+\log r_{*}O(r_{*}^{-3}),

with
\Lambda_{m\ell}(\omega)=\lambda_{m\ell}(\omega)+a^{2}\omega^{2}
, where
\lambda_{m\ell}(\omega)
are oblate spheroidal eigenvalues.

In comparison, the potential
\widetilde{V}_{\ell}
introduced in §
1.2.1
has the following behaviour around
r=r_{+}=1
:

\widetilde{V}_{\ell\omega}(r)=2{q}\widetilde{\omega}r_{*}^{-1}+[\ell(\ell+1)-q^{2}+O(\widetilde{\omega})]r_{*}^{-2}+\log r_{*}O(r_{*}^{-3})

Under the transformation
am\mapsto q
and
\Lambda_{m\ell}(\frac{m}{2})-2m^{2}\mapsto\ell(\ell+1)-q^{2}
, the asymptotic behaviours of the potentials as
r_{*}\to-\infty
and
\widetilde{\omega}\to 0
agree.

In fact, the analogue of the potential
\mathcal{V}_{\widehat{\omega}\ell}(r)
in extremal Kerr has the same structure as
\mathcal{V}_{\widehat{\omega}\ell}(r)
in the setting of the charged scalar field, with the above identifications for bounded
r
.

The analysis concerning the radial ODE in a neighbourhood of
r=r_{+}
developed in the present paper applies therefore also to the extremal Kerr case. Since we allow the constants in the relevant estimates to depend on
q
, the analysis is only applicable in the setting of bounded
m
and can be used to remove the conditional assumptions in the integrated energy estimates in
[
Gaj23a
]
.

The improvement of the results in
[
Gaj23a
]
as well as the consideration of unbounded
m
is part of future work.

1.4.
Overview of the remainder of the paper

Here, we provide an outline of the content of the remaining sections of the paper.

•

In §
2
we provide all the necessary geometric preliminaries and we set notational conventions. We moreover derive different forms of (
1.2
).

•

We give a precise statement of the main theorem of the paper in §
3
.

•

The analysis in §
4
concerns small
|\mathfrak{q}Q|
and is purely physical-space based and independent from the Fourier analysis in the rest of the paper.

•

In §
5
we explain how to relate the analysis of (
1.2
) to the analysis of an appropriate Schrödinger ODE, by taking a Fourier transform.

•

We carry out the high-frequency analysis in §
5
.

•

In §
7
, we perform the bounded-frequency analysis. This section may be thought of as the core of the paper and it contains most of the new ideas.

•

We put together the high- and bounded-frequency analysis in §
8
and then apply Plancherel’s theorem to obtain physical space estimates in §
9
.

•

The appendix contains some results that can be read independently from the rest of the paper. In Appendix
A
we provide a purely physical-space proof of integrated energy decay estimates, with zeroth order terms on the right-hand side. This result is also used in the small-
|\mathfrak{q}Q|
-analysis. In Appendix
B
, we prove a restricted mode stability result away from extremality. This result is not used in the rest of the paper.

•

Finally, Appendix
C
contains ODE tools that are necessary for carrying out the matched asymptotic analysis in §
7
.

1.5.
Acknowledgments

We thank Marc Casals for helpful discussions and for sharing relevant computations and Rita Teixeira da Costa for invaluable discussions on matched asymptotics in a wider context. The author acknowledges funding through the ERC Starting Grant 101115568.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section, we introduce the relevant geometric concepts, as well as the charged scalar field equation. We also briefly discuss local energy estimates and introduce notation that will be used throughout the remaining sections of the paper and is necessary to be able to give a precise statement of the main theorems in §
3
.

2.1.
Reissner–Nordström–de Sitter spacetimes

Consider the parameters
(M,Q,\Lambda)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)
and the corresponding polynomial

r^{2}\Omega^{2}(r):=r^{2}-2Mr+Q^{2}-\frac{\Lambda}{3}r^{4}.

For
Q\neq 0
,
r^{2}\Omega^{2}(r)
has up to three positive roots
r_{-},r_{+},r_{c}
, with

0<r_{-}\leq r_{+}\leq r_{c}\leq\infty,

where we take the convention that
r_{c}=\infty
if
\Lambda=0
. We will restrict to
M^{2}\Lambda
sufficiently small, so that
r_{+}<r_{c}
. Later in the paper, we will restrict further to
M^{2}\Lambda\ll 1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{M,Q,\Lambda}=\mathbb{R}_{v}\times[r_{+},r_{c})_{r}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}_{(\theta,\varphi)}
denote the
Reissner–Nordström(–de Sitter) exterior manifold with boundary
.
The
Reissner–Nordström(–de Sitter) exterior spacetimes
are tuples
(\mathcal{M}_{M,Q,\Lambda},g_{M,Q,\Lambda})
(together with the time orientation determined by the vector field
\partial_{v}
), with
g_{M,Q,\Lambda}
a Lorentzian metric on
\mathcal{M}_{M,Q,\Lambda}
that can be expressed as follows:

\displaystyle g_{M,Q,\Lambda}=

\displaystyle-\Omega^{2}dv^{2}+2dvdr+r^{2}\not{g}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}},

(2.1)

\displaystyle\not{g}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}:=

\displaystyle\>d\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta d\varphi^{2},

where
v\in\mathbb{R}
and
r\in[r_{+},\infty)
are called
ingoing Eddington–Finkelstein coordinates
.
9
9

9

The spherical coordinate chart
(\theta,\varphi)
feature a degeneration at a half-circle connecting the north and south pole and can be complemented with an additional coordinate chart
(\tilde{\theta},\tilde{\varphi})
to cover the full sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.

When
\Lambda=0
, the spacetimes are called
Reissner–Nordström
spacetimes. When
\Lambda>0
, they are called
Reissner–Nordström–de Sitter
spacetimes.

The
future event horizon

\mathcal{H}^{+}
is defined as the boundary:

\mathcal{H}^{+}:=\partial\mathcal{M}_{M,Q,\Lambda}=\{r=r_{+}\}.

The corresponding inverse or dual metric is:

g_{M,Q,\Lambda}^{-1}=\Omega^{2}\partial_{r}\otimes\partial_{r}+\partial_{v}\otimes\partial_{r}+\partial_{r}\otimes\partial_{v}+r^{-2}\not{g}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{-1},

with
\not{g}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{-1}
the inverse metric associated to the unit round sphere metric
\not{g}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}
.

The
event horizon surface gravity

\kappa_{+}
and the
cosmological horizon surface gravity

\kappa_{c}
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle\kappa_{+}(Q,M,\Lambda):=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d\Omega^{2}}{dr}(r_{+}),

\displaystyle\kappa_{c}(Q,M,\Lambda):=-\frac{1}{2}\lim_{r\uparrow r_{c}}\frac{d\Omega^{2}}{dr}(r).

From the lemma below, it follows that
\kappa_{c}\downarrow 0
as
\Lambda\downarrow 0
. When
\kappa_{+}=0
, we will say the spacetime is
extremal
.
10
10

10

There are another notions of extremality in the Reissner–Nordström–de Sitter context, which arise when
r_{+}=r_{c}
; see for example the discussion in
[
Gaj18
]
[§1]. These spacetimes do not play a role in the present paper, since we assumed the strict inequality
r_{+}<r_{c}
.
In particular, when
\kappa_{c}=\kappa_{+}=0
, we have that
r_{+}=r_{-}
and
r_{c}=\infty
and the spacetimes are called
extremal Reissner–Nordström
spacetimes. In that case,
|Q|=M
.

When
\kappa_{+}>0
and
\kappa_{c}\geq 0
, we say the spacetimes are
sub-extremal
. If in addition
0<M\kappa_{+}\ll 1
and
0\leq M\kappa_{c}\ll 1
, we say the sub-extremal spacetimes are
near-extremal
.

In the lemma below, we relate the parameters
(M,\Lambda,Q)
to the surface gravities
(\kappa_{+},\kappa_{c})
.

Lemma 2.1
.

(i)

We can write:

r^{2}\Omega^{2}(r)=\frac{\Lambda}{3}(r_{c}-r)(r-r_{+})(r-r_{-})(r+r_{c}+r_{+}+r_{-}),

with

\displaystyle\frac{3}{\Lambda}=

\displaystyle\>r_{c}^{2}+r_{+}^{2}+r_{-}^{2}+r_{+}r_{-}+r_{c}r_{+}+r_{c}r_{-},

\displaystyle 2M\cdot\frac{3}{\Lambda}=

\displaystyle\>r_{c}^{2}(r_{+}+r_{-})+r_{+}^{2}(r_{c}+r_{+})+r_{-}^{2}(r_{c}+r_{-})+2r_{c}r_{+}r_{-},

\displaystyle Q^{2}\cdot\frac{3}{\Lambda}=

\displaystyle\>(r_{c}+r_{+}+r_{-})r_{c}r_{+}r_{-}.

Furthermore, we can express:

\displaystyle M=

\displaystyle\>r_{+}\left(1-\kappa_{+}r_{+}-\frac{2\Lambda}{3}r_{+}^{2}\right)=r_{c}\left(1-\kappa_{c}r_{c}-\frac{2\Lambda}{3}r_{c}^{2}\right),

\displaystyle Q^{2}=

\displaystyle\>r_{+}^{2}\left(1-2\kappa_{+}r_{+}-\Lambda r_{+}^{2}\right)=r_{c}^{2}\left(1-2\kappa_{c}r_{c}-\Lambda r_{c}^{2}\right).

(ii)

We can express:

\displaystyle\kappa_{+}=

\displaystyle\>\frac{\Lambda}{6}r_{+}^{-2}(r_{c}-r_{+})(r_{+}-r_{-})(2r_{+}+r_{c}+r_{-}),

\displaystyle\kappa_{c}=

\displaystyle\>\frac{\Lambda}{6}r_{c}^{-2}(r_{c}-r_{+})(r_{c}-r_{-})(2r_{c}+r_{+}+r_{-}).

(iii)

Fix
Q,M>0
. Then

\displaystyle\lim_{\Lambda\downarrow 0}\frac{\Lambda}{3}r_{c}^{2}=

\displaystyle\>1,

\displaystyle\lim_{\Lambda\downarrow 0}r_{c}\kappa_{c}(Q,M,\Lambda)=

\displaystyle\>1,

\displaystyle\lim_{\Lambda\downarrow 0}\kappa_{+}(Q,M,\Lambda)=

\displaystyle\>\frac{r_{+}-r_{-}}{2r_{+}^{2}}.

(iv)

We can expand:

\displaystyle\frac{\Lambda}{3}r_{c}^{2}=

\displaystyle\>1-(r_{+}+r_{-})\kappa_{c}+r_{+}O(\kappa_{c}),

\displaystyle\kappa_{c}r_{c}=

\displaystyle\>1-\frac{3}{2}(r_{+}+r_{-})\kappa_{c}+r_{+}^{2}O(\kappa_{c}^{2}).

Proof.

Straightforward consequences of expanding the following identity, which uses that
r^{2}\Omega^{2}
is a fourth-order polynomial:

C_{0}(r_{c}-r)(r-r_{+})(r-r_{-})(r-r_{0})=r^{2}\Omega^{2}(r)=r^{2}-2Mr+Q^{2}-\frac{\Lambda}{3}r^{4}.\qed

Furthermore, the expressions for
M
and
Q
in terms of
\kappa_{+}
,
r_{+}
and
\Lambda
follow from solving the system:

\displaystyle\Omega^{2}(r_{+})=

\displaystyle\>0,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d\Omega}{dr}(r_{+})=

\displaystyle\>\kappa_{+}.

Similarly, the expressions for
M
and
Q
in terms of
\kappa_{c}
,
r_{c}
and
\Lambda
follow from
\Omega^{2}(r_{+})=0
and
\frac{1}{2}\frac{d\Omega}{dr}(r_{c})=-\kappa_{c}
.

It will be convenient to characterize the Reissner–Nordström spacetimes via the triple
(r_{+},\kappa_{+},\kappa_{c})
instead of
(\Lambda,M,Q)
. In much of the analysis, we will also simplify the notation by fixing, without loss of generality,
r_{+}=1
.

2.2.
Notation: inequalities and Big-O notation

We first introduce the following notation. Let
h:I\to\mathbb{R}
be a non-polynomial function. We use
O_{s}(h)
to group all functions
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying the following estimate: let
s,l\in(0,\infty]
, with
l\leq s
. Then there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
x\in I
and
l\leq s
:

\left|\frac{d^{l}f}{dx^{l}}\right|(x)\leq C\left|\frac{d^{l}h}{dx^{l}}\right|(x).

We write
O(h)=O_{0}(h)
and when the above estimate holds for any
s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we write
O_{\infty}(h)
.

We extend the above notation to polynomial
h
as follows. We write
O_{s}(x^{m})
for
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, if

\left|\frac{d^{l}f}{dx^{l}}\right|(x)\leq Cx^{-m-l}

for all
l\leq s
. When
m=0
, we write
O_{s}(x^{0})
to group functions
f
satisfying:

\left|\frac{d^{l}f}{dx^{l}}\right|(x)\leq Cx^{-l}

for all
l\leq s
.

When the constant
C
is not entirely uniform and depends on an additional parameter
\lambda
, we will place the parameter in the superscript and write
O_{s}^{\lambda}(h)
.

Furthermore, given a constant
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we also use the notation
O(\alpha^{s})
to group
constants

\beta\in\mathbb{C}
satisfying
|\beta|\leq C|\alpha|^{s}
, with
C
independent of
\alpha
.

We will use
c,C
to denote constants appearing in inequalities for functions
f
that are
uniform
in the following sense:
c,C
only depend on
r_{+}
, the choice of foliation defining function
\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}
, or equivalently
\mathbbm{h}
, introduced in §
2.4
and
q=\mathfrak{q}Q
, the charge parameter introduced in §
2.8
. If
c
or
C
have additional dependencies, we will denote them explicitly. To ease the notion in the paper, we will also adhere to the following convention (the “algebra of constants”):

C\cdot C=C,\quad c\cdot c=c,\quad c^{-1}=C,\quad\quad C+C=C,\quad c+c=c,\quad C+c=C.

Furthermore, we will use the notation
f\lesssim h
when
|f|\leq C|h|
,
f\gtrsim h
when
|f|\geq c|h|
and
f\sim h
when
f\lesssim h
and
f\gtrsim h
.

2.3.
Properties of
r^{-2}\Omega^{2}

In the lemma below, we will describe the asymptotic behaviour of
r^{-2}\Omega^{2}
on near-extremal Reissner–Nordström–de Sitter spacetimes. For this purpose, it is convenient to introduce the following alternative radial coordinates:

\displaystyle\rho_{+}:=

\displaystyle\>r_{+}^{-1}-r^{-1},

\displaystyle\rho_{c}:=

\displaystyle\>r^{-1}-r_{c}^{-1}.

Note that
\rho_{+},\rho_{c}\in[0,r_{+}^{-1}-r_{c}^{-1})
.

Lemma 2.2
.

The following estimates hold:

\displaystyle r^{-2}\Omega^{2}(r(\rho_{+}))=

\displaystyle\>2\kappa_{+}\rho_{+}+(1-6\kappa_{+}r_{+}-2\Lambda r_{+}^{2})\rho_{+}^{2}+(-2r_{+}+6\kappa_{+}r_{+}^{2}+\frac{8}{3}\Lambda r_{+}^{3})\rho_{+}^{3}

\displaystyle+r_{+}^{2}\left(1-2\kappa_{+}r_{+}-\Lambda r_{+}^{2}\right)\rho_{+}^{4},

\displaystyle\frac{d(r^{-2}\Omega^{2})}{d\rho_{+}}(r(\rho_{+}))=

\displaystyle\>2\kappa_{+}+2\left[1-6r_{+}\kappa_{+}-2\Lambda r_{+}^{2}\right]\rho_{+}+2(-3r_{+}+9\kappa_{+}r_{+}^{2}+4\Lambda r_{+}^{3})\rho_{+}^{2}

\displaystyle+4r_{+}^{2}\left(1-2\kappa_{+}r_{+}-\Lambda r_{+}^{2}\right)\rho_{+}^{4},

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}(r^{-2}\Omega^{2})}{d\rho_{+}^{2}}(r(\rho_{+}))=

\displaystyle\>2\left[1-6r_{+}\kappa_{+}-2\Lambda r_{+}^{2}\right]-4(3r_{+}-9\kappa_{+}r_{+}^{2}-4\Lambda r_{+}^{3})\rho_{+}

\displaystyle+12r_{+}^{2}\left(1-2\kappa_{+}r_{+}-\Lambda r_{+}^{2}\right)\rho_{+}^{4},

\displaystyle r^{-2}\Omega^{2}(r(\rho_{c}))=

\displaystyle\>2\kappa_{c}\rho_{c}+(1+6\kappa_{c}r_{c}-2\Lambda r_{c}^{2})\rho_{c}^{2}+(2r_{c}+6\kappa_{c}r_{c}^{2}-\frac{8}{3}\Lambda r_{c}^{3})\rho_{c}^{3}

\displaystyle+r_{+}^{2}\left(1-2\kappa_{+}r_{+}-\Lambda r_{+}^{2}\right)\rho_{c}^{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\>2\kappa_{c}\rho_{c}+(1+O(\kappa_{c}))\rho_{c}^{2}+(-2r_{+}+2\kappa_{+}r_{+}^{2}+O(\kappa_{c}))\rho_{c}^{3}

\displaystyle+r_{+}^{2}\left(1-2\kappa_{+}r_{+}-\Lambda r_{+}^{2}\right)\rho_{c}^{4},

\displaystyle\frac{d(r^{-2}\Omega^{2})}{d\rho_{c}}(r(\rho_{c}))=

\displaystyle\>2\kappa_{c}+2\left[1+6r_{c}\kappa_{c}-2\Lambda r_{c}^{2}\right]\rho_{c}+2(3r_{c}+9\kappa_{c}r_{c}^{2}

\displaystyle+4r_{+}^{2}\left(1-2\kappa_{+}r_{+}-\Lambda r_{+}^{2}\right)\rho_{c}^{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\>2\kappa_{c}+2(1+O(\kappa_{c}))\rho_{c}+3(-2r_{+}+2\kappa_{+}r_{+}^{2}+O(\kappa_{c}))\rho_{c}^{2}

\displaystyle+4r_{+}^{2}\left(1-2\kappa_{+}r_{+}-\Lambda r_{+}^{2}\right)\rho_{c}^{4},

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}(r^{-2}\Omega^{2})}{d\rho_{c}^{2}}(r(\rho_{c}))=

\displaystyle\>2\left[1+6r_{c}\kappa_{c}-2\Lambda r_{c}^{2}\right]+4(3r_{c}+9\kappa_{c}r_{c}^{2}-4\Lambda r_{c}^{3})\rho_{c}

\displaystyle+12r_{+}^{2}\left(1-2\kappa_{+}r_{+}-\Lambda r_{+}^{2}\right)\rho_{c}^{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\>2(1+O(\kappa_{c}))+4(-3r_{+}+3\kappa_{+}r_{+}^{2}+O(\kappa_{c}))\rho_{c}+12r_{+}^{2}\left(1-2\kappa_{+}r_{+}-\Lambda r_{+}^{2}\right)\rho_{c}^{4}.

Proof.

Follows from a Taylor expansion of
r^{-2}\Omega^{2}
in
\rho_{+}
or
\rho_{c}
, combined with the expressions for
\kappa_{+}
,
\kappa_{c}

M
and
Q
in Lemma
2.1
.
∎

The function
\frac{d}{dr}\left(r^{-2}\Omega^{2}\right)
has a single root
r=r_{\sharp}
in
(r_{+},r_{c})
, with

r_{\sharp}=\frac{3M}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{9M^{2}-8Q^{2}}.

The hypersurface
\{r=r_{\sharp}\}
is called the
photon sphere
. Observe that it does not depend on
\Lambda
.

We define the
tortoise coordinate

r_{*}:(r_{+},r_{c})\to\mathbb{R}
as the solution to the ODE:

\displaystyle\frac{dr_{*}}{dr}=

\displaystyle\>\frac{1}{\Omega^{2}},

\displaystyle r_{*}(r_{\sharp})=

\displaystyle\>0.

We can write

\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}(\rho_{+})=\frac{1}{\rho_{+}(\rho_{+}+2\kappa_{+})}(1+O_{\infty}(r_{+}\rho_{+})+\frac{r_{+}\kappa_{c}^{2}}{\kappa_{+}}O_{\infty}(r_{+}\rho_{+})).

The function
r_{*}
therefore satisfies the following asymptotic properties:

r_{*}(r(\rho_{+}))=\int_{r_{0}}^{r(\rho_{+})}\frac{1}{\Omega^{2}}(r^{\prime})\,dr^{\prime}=\int_{\rho_{+}(r_{0})}^{\rho_{+}}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\right)(r(\rho_{+}^{\prime}))\,d\rho_{+}^{\prime}\\
=\int_{\rho_{+}(r_{0})}^{\rho_{+}}\frac{1}{\rho_{+}^{\prime}(\rho_{+}^{\prime}+2\kappa_{+})}(1+O_{\infty}(r_{+}\rho_{+}^{\prime})+\frac{r_{+}\kappa_{c}^{2}}{\kappa_{+}}O_{\infty}(r_{+}\rho_{+}^{\prime}))\,d\rho_{+}^{\prime}\\
=\frac{1}{2\kappa_{+}}\log\left(\frac{\rho_{+}}{\rho_{+}+2\kappa_{+}}\right)+\log(\rho_{+}+2\kappa_{+})\left[O_{\infty}((r_{+}\rho_{+})^{0})+\frac{r_{+}\kappa_{c}^{2}}{\kappa_{+}}O_{\infty}((r_{+}\rho_{+})^{0})\right].

(2.2)

When
\kappa_{c}\leq\kappa_{+}<\rho_{+}
, we can expand further to obtain

r_{*}(r(\rho_{+}))=-\rho_{+}^{-1}\left[1+O_{\infty}(\kappa_{+}\rho_{+}^{-1})\right]+r_{+}\log(r_{+}\rho_{+})O((r_{+}\rho_{+})^{0}).

(2.3)

When
\kappa_{c}\leq\kappa_{+}
and
\rho_{+}<\kappa_{+}
, we can expand further to obtain

r_{*}(r(\rho_{+}))=-\frac{1}{2\kappa_{+}}\left[\log(2\kappa_{+}\rho_{+}^{-1})+O_{\infty}(\kappa_{+}^{-1}\rho_{+})^{0}\right].

(2.4)

We repeat the above computations with the role of
\rho_{+}
taken on by
\rho_{c}
to obtain:

r_{*}(r(\rho_{c}))=-\frac{1}{2\kappa_{c}}\log\left(\frac{\rho_{c}}{\rho_{c}+2\kappa_{c}}\right)+\log(\rho_{c}+2\kappa_{c})O_{\infty}((r_{+}\rho_{c})^{0}).

(2.5)

When
\kappa_{c}<\rho_{c}
, we can expand further to obtain

r_{*}(r(\rho_{c}))=\rho_{c}^{-1}\left[1+O_{\infty}(\kappa_{c}\rho_{c}^{-1})\right]+r_{+}\log(r_{+}\rho_{c})O((r_{+}\rho_{+})^{0}).

(2.6)

When
\rho_{c}<\kappa_{c}
, we can expand further to obtain

r_{*}(r(\rho_{c}))=\frac{1}{2\kappa_{c}}\left[\log(2\kappa_{c}\rho_{c}^{-1})+O_{\infty}(\kappa_{c}^{-1}\rho_{+})^{0}\right]

(2.7)

We conclude in particular that in all cases
r_{*}((r_{+},r_{c}))=\mathbb{R}
.

Let
t=v-r_{*}
. Then the metric
g_{M,Q,\Lambda}
takes the following form in the interior of
\mathcal{M}_{M,Q,\Lambda}
,
\mathring{\mathcal{M}}_{M,Q,\Lambda}=\mathbb{R}_{v}\times(r_{+},r_{c})_{r}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
, with respect to
(t,r,\theta,\varphi)
coordinates:

g_{M,Q,\Lambda}=-\Omega^{2}dt^{2}+\Omega^{-2}dr^{2}+r^{2}(d\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta d\varphi^{2}).

2.4.
Foliations

We will foliate
\mathcal{M}_{M,Q,\Lambda}
by spacelike hypersurfaces
\Sigma_{\tau}
, where
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
. To construct
\Sigma_{\tau}
, we first introduce the foliation-defining function
\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}:[r_{+},r_{c})\to[0,\infty)
, which is a smooth function satisfying:

2-\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}\Omega^{2}\geq 0.

We moreover define
\mathbbm{h}:[r_{+},r_{c})\to[0,\infty)
as follows:

\mathbbm{h}(r):=2-\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}\Omega^{2}(r).

The time function
\tau:\mathcal{M}_{M,Q,\Lambda}\to\mathbb{R}
is then defined as follows:

\tau(v,r,\theta,\varphi)=v-\int_{r_{+}}^{r}\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}(r^{\prime})\,dr^{\prime}.

We denote the level sets of constant
\tau
by
\Sigma_{\tau}
. Since

g^{-1}_{M,Q,\Lambda}(d\tau,d\tau)=-\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}\mathbbm{h}\leq 0,

we have that
\Sigma_{\tau}
must consist of spacelike or null segments. Furthermore, if
\mathbbm{h}>0
and
\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}>0
, then
\Sigma_{\tau}
are smooth spacelike hypersurfaces for all
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
.

We record the following identity that we will appeal to later:

\Omega^{2}\mathbbm{h}\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}=1-(1-\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}\Omega^{2})^{2}.

(2.8)

We can express:

g_{M,Q,\Lambda}^{-1}=-\mathbbm{h}\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}\partial_{\tau}\otimes\partial_{\tau}+(1-\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}\Omega^{2})(\partial_{\tau}\otimes\partial_{r}+\partial_{r}\otimes\partial_{\tau})+\Omega^{2}\partial_{r}\otimes\partial_{r}+r^{-2}\not{g}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{-1}.

(2.9)

By applying (
2.8
), it then follows that
\sqrt{-\det g_{M,Q,\Lambda}}=r^{2}\sin\theta
.

Note that
v|_{\Sigma_{\tau}}(r_{+})=\tau
, so
\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}=\{r=r_{+}\}\cap\{v=\tau\}
. Define
u(\tau,r):=v(\tau,r)-2r_{*}
. Then
u(\tau,r_{\sharp})=v(\tau,r_{\sharp})=\tau+\int_{r_{+}}^{r_{\sharp}}\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}(r^{\prime})\,dr^{\prime}
. To conclude that the restriction
\left|u|_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\right|(r)
is bounded as
r\to r_{c}
, we express:

u(\tau,r)=\tau+\int_{r_{\sharp}}^{r}(\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}-2\Omega^{-2})(r^{\prime})\,dr^{\prime}+\int_{r_{+}}^{r_{\sharp}}\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}(r^{\prime})\,dr^{\prime}=\tau-\int_{r_{\sharp}}^{r}(\Omega^{-2}\mathbbm{h})(r^{\prime})\,dr^{\prime}+\int_{r_{+}}^{r_{\sharp}}\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}(r^{\prime})\,dr^{\prime}.

Hence,
\lim_{r\to r_{c}}u(\tau,r)
is well-defined if
\Omega^{-2}\mathbbm{h}
is integrable on
[r_{\sharp},r_{c})
. For the sake of convenience, we will occasionally make one of the two following assumptions:

(1)

Either there exists
h_{0}>0
such that for
r\geq r_{\sharp}
:

\Omega^{-2}\mathbbm{h}(r)=h_{0}r^{-2}+O_{\infty}(r^{-3}),

(2)

Or there exist
r_{+}<r_{H}<r_{I}<r_{c}
, such that
\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}(r)=0
when
r\leq r_{H}
and
\mathbbm{h}(r)=0
when
r\geq r_{I}
.

Finally, it will be convenient to express the metric in
(u,x,\theta,\varphi)
coordinates, with
x=\frac{1}{r}
:

g=r^{2}(\Omega^{2}x^{2}du^{2}+2dudx+\not{g}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}).

We can express with respect to
(u,x)
coordinates:
\mathcal{M}_{M,Q,\Lambda}\cong\mathcal{H}^{+}\cup\mathbb{R}_{u}\times(0,r_{+}^{-1})_{x}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Then we extend
\mathcal{M}_{M,Q,\Lambda}
by defining:

\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{M,Q,\Lambda}:=\mathcal{H}^{+}\cup(\mathbb{R}_{u}\times[0,r_{+}^{-1})_{x}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}).

We denote the level set
\{x=0\}
of
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{M,Q,\Lambda}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{+}:=

\displaystyle\>\{x=0\}\subset\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{M,Q,\Lambda}\quad(\Lambda=0),

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{+}:=

\displaystyle\>\{x=0\}\subset\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{M,Q,\Lambda}\quad(\Lambda>0).

We refer to
\mathcal{I}^{+}
as
future null infinity
and to
\mathcal{C}^{+}
as the
cosmological horizon
.

When integrating over the unit round sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, we will make use of the notation:

d\sigma:=\sin\theta d\theta d\varphi.

2.5.
Couch–Torrence radial coordinates

The following alternative radial coordinates will be convenient to make manifest symmetries between the event and cosmological horizons:

\displaystyle s_{+}:=

\displaystyle\>\frac{1}{\rho_{+}}-r_{+},

\displaystyle s_{c}:=

\displaystyle\>\frac{1}{\rho_{c}}-r_{+}.

Since
\rho_{+},\rho_{c}\in[0,r_{+}^{-1}-r_{c}^{-1})
, we have that
s_{+},s_{c}\in(\frac{r_{+}^{2}}{r_{c}-r_{+}},\infty)
. When
\kappa_{c}=0
, we have that
s_{c}=r-r_{+}
and
s_{+}=\frac{r_{+}^{2}}{r-r_{+}}
.

Furthermore, we can express:

\displaystyle r_{+}^{-1}s_{c}=

\displaystyle\>\frac{1+r_{+}r_{c}^{-1}(r_{+}^{-1}s_{+}+r_{+}r_{c}^{-1})}{(1-r_{+}r_{c}^{-1})r_{+}^{-1}s_{+}+r_{+}^{2}r_{c}^{-2}},

(2.10)

\displaystyle r_{+}^{-1}s_{+}=

\displaystyle\>\frac{1+(r_{+}r_{c}^{-1})^{2}(1-r_{+}^{-1}s_{c})}{(1-r_{+}r_{c}^{-1})r_{+}^{-1}s_{c}-r_{+}r_{c}^{-1}}.

(2.11)

We will refer to
s_{+}
and
s_{c}
as
Couch–Torrence radial coordinates
and the map
s_{c}\mapsto s_{+}
as a
Couch–Torrence transformation
, see
[
CT84
]
. This map is equivalent to
\rho_{c}\mapsto\rho_{+}
.

As the lemma below shows, the map
s_{c}\mapsto s_{+}
plays a special role in the extremal case
\kappa_{c}=\kappa_{+}=0
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\kappa_{c}=\kappa_{+}=0
and
r_{+}=1
. Consider the functions
r,\Omega,r_{*}:[0,\infty)\to[1,\infty)
, which are defined as follows:
r(s_{c}):=s_{c}+1
,
\Omega(s_{c}):=(s_{c}^{-1}+1)^{-1}
and
r_{*}(s_{c}):=\int_{1}^{s_{c}}\frac{1}{\Omega^{2}(s_{c})}\,ds_{c}
. Then

\displaystyle\Omega(s_{+})=

\displaystyle\>\frac{1}{r(s_{c})},

(2.12)

\displaystyle\Omega(s_{c})=

\displaystyle\>\frac{1}{r(s_{+})},

(2.13)

\displaystyle\left(r^{-1}\Omega^{2}\frac{d\Omega^{2}}{dr}\right)(s_{c})=

\displaystyle\>\left(r^{-1}\Omega^{2}\frac{d\Omega^{2}}{dr}\right)(s_{+}),

(2.14)

\displaystyle r_{*}(s_{c})=

\displaystyle\>-r_{*}(s_{+}(s_{c})).

(2.15)

Proof.

The identities (
2.12
) and (
2.13
) are immediate consequences of the identity
s_{+}=s_{c}^{-1}
. The identity (
2.14
) similarly follows straightforwardly.

We then use that

r_{*}(s_{c})=-\int_{1}^{s_{+}(s_{c})}\frac{1}{\Omega^{2}(s_{c}(s_{+}))}s_{+}^{-2}\,ds_{+}=-\int_{1}^{s_{+}(s_{c})}\frac{1}{\Omega^{2}(s_{+})}\,ds_{+}

to conclude (
2.15
).
∎

In fact, from the above lemma, it can easily be shown that the map
(t,s_{c},\theta,\varphi)\mapsto(t,s_{+},\theta,\varphi)
is a conformal isometry of
(\mathring{\mathcal{M}}_{M,M,0},g_{M,M,0})
.

2.6.
Key vector fields

The following vector fields will play key roles in the analysis in the remainder of the article. With respect to
(v,r,\theta,\varphi)
coordinates:

\displaystyle T:=

\displaystyle\>\partial_{v},

\displaystyle X:=

\displaystyle\>\partial_{r}+\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}\partial_{v},

\displaystyle\underline{L}:=

\displaystyle\>-\frac{\Omega^{2}}{2}\partial_{r},

\displaystyle L:=

\displaystyle\>T-\underline{L},

\displaystyle Y_{*}:=

\displaystyle L-\underline{L}=\Omega^{2}X+(1-\widetilde{\mathbbm{h}}\Omega^{2})T.

With respect to
(\tau,r,\theta,\varphi)
coordinates, we have that
T=\partial_{\tau}
and
X=\partial_{r}
.

We will also denote for
q\in\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle K_{+}:=

\displaystyle\>T+iqr_{+}^{-1}\mathbf{1},

\displaystyle K_{c}:=

\displaystyle\>T+iqr_{c}^{-1}\mathbf{1}.

where we will fix
q
via (
2.17
) in §
2.8
.

2.7.
Frequency decompositions on
\mathbb{S}^{2}

We can decompose any function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2};\mathbb{C})
as follows:

f=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z},|m|\leq\ell}f_{\ell m}Y_{\ell m},

with
f_{\ell m}\in\mathbb{C}
and
Y_{\ell m}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
the
(\ell,m)
-th spherical harmonics, which satisfy
\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}Y_{\ell m}=-\ell(\ell+1)Y_{\ell m}
and
\langle Y_{\ell m},Y_{\ell^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}=\delta_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}\delta_{mm^{\prime}}
.

We moreover denote:

\displaystyle f_{\ell}=

\displaystyle\>\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z},|m|\leq\ell}f_{\ell m}Y_{\ell m},

\displaystyle f_{\geq\ell}=

\displaystyle\>\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=\ell}^{\infty}f_{\ell^{\prime}}.

2.8.
